Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.

Honestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.

So… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying with things I really need to focus on. Like at work, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else completely.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or suddenly realize I missed the whole point of what people were discussing. And these aren’t unimportant things — I manage health services, so I need to be tracking this stuff. I mean, I’ve always had a lot going on, and meetings can be dry, sure, but lately it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting me enough that I thought I should finally talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and then that can make me feel kind of low for a bit, but not like… sad all the time. It’s more that I’m annoyed because I know I’m capable, and then I’ll lose track in a meeting and spend the rest of the afternoon thinking about it.\n\nActually, yesterday I was trying to clean up my inbox after one of those long metrics calls, and I found an email I meant to answer two weeks ago, which just sent me down this whole rabbit hole of checking other messages. But, sorry — to answer you, I haven’t really noticed feeling depressed or down in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as restless, exactly. I mean, I’ll shift around in my chair sometimes, but I think everyone does that, especially in those long conference room chairs that somehow manage to be uncomfortable no matter how you sit.\n\nI haven’t really noticed a big urge to get up and move around or anything like that. It’s more like… well, I don’t know, I’d have to think about it. Sitting still isn’t usually the thing I notice first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks that you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, not every single thing, but it happens enough that it’s a problem.\n\nFor example, after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up items — like send one email, update a spreadsheet, submit something by the end of the week. I’ll usually do the first one, and maybe I’ll even start the second, like draft the email or open the spreadsheet… and then somehow it just sits there unfinished.\n\nAnd then a few days later, or sometimes longer, someone reminds me and I’m like, “Oh no, I was halfway through that.” It’s embarrassing because these are routine work things, not huge impossible projects.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or completing tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does happen outside of work too, though I notice it most at work because people are waiting on things.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less formal. Like I’ll start something with a few steps, and I’ll do the first part, maybe get the next part half set up, and then it just sort of stays there. It’s the same pattern as after meetings — if someone gives me three or four follow-up steps, I might send the first email or start the spreadsheet, but then the rest sits unfinished until somebody reminds me.\n\nAnd then I feel bad because it’s not that I didn’t care. I just… I don’t know, I lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive, like I’m not usually the person who just suddenly does something wild without considering it.\n\nI mean, I might interrupt sometimes or buy something online because I’ve been meaning to replace it anyway—although half the time I spend way too long comparing reviews, which is its own whole thing. My sister can decide on a couch in ten minutes; I’ll have twelve tabs open and still not know.\n\nBut acting without thinking things through… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I use a calendar, and I make lists—probably too many lists, honestly. I have one on my phone, one on sticky notes, and then sometimes I’ll write something down on the back of an envelope and of course that disappears.\n\nBut whether that means I have trouble organizing time or tasks… maybe? I guess it depends what you mean. Like, if it’s a workday, there are meetings already on the calendar, so that sort of organizes me from the outside. And then I’ll have these little windows between meetings where I think, okay, I’ll knock out two things, but then I end up reorganizing my inbox or looking for the right version of a document.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I do try to organize things. I just don’t always know if my system is actually working or if I’m just constantly trying to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, stress probably makes it worse, but I don’t think it only happens when I’m overwhelmed.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is stuff like, I’ll open an EPIC quality report with every intention of really going through it, or I’ll be sitting in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow the discussion, and within a few minutes my mind is just… gone somewhere else. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. But the drifting happens even when I start out calm and prepared.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus on certain types of tasks or activities than others?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.

Relaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.

My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

So… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.

And these aren’t unimportant things — I manage health services, so I need to be tracking this stuff. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and then that can make me feel kind of low for a bit, but not like… sad all the time. It’s more that I’m annoyed because I know I’m capable, and then I’ll lose track in a meeting and spend the rest of the afternoon thinking about it.\n\nActually, yesterday I was trying to clean up my inbox after one of those long metrics calls, and I found an email I meant to answer two weeks ago, which just sent me down this whole rabbit hole of checking other messages. But, sorry — to answer you, I haven’t really noticed feeling depressed or down in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as restless, exactly. It’s more like… well, I don’t know, I’d have to think about it. I mean, not every single thing, but it happens enough that it’s a problem.\n\nFor example, after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up items — like send one email, update a spreadsheet, submit something by the end of the week. I’ll usually do the first one, and maybe I’ll even start the second, like draft the email or open the spreadsheet… and then somehow it just sits there unfinished.\n\nAnd then a few days later, or sometimes longer, someone reminds me and I’m like, “Oh no, I was halfway through that.” It’s embarrassing because these are routine work things, not huge impossible projects.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or completing tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does happen outside of work too, though I notice it most at work because people are waiting on things.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less formal. Like I’ll start something with a few steps, and I’ll do the first part, maybe get the next part half set up, and then it just sort of stays there. It’s the same pattern as after meetings — if someone gives me three or four follow-up steps, I might send the first email or start the spreadsheet, but then the rest sits unfinished until somebody reminds me.\n\nAnd then I feel bad because it’s not that I didn’t care. I just… I don’t know, I lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive, like I’m not usually the person who just suddenly does something wild without considering it.\n\nI mean, I might interrupt sometimes or buy something online because I’ve been meaning to replace it anyway—although half the time I spend way too long comparing reviews, which is its own whole thing. My sister can decide on a couch in ten minutes; I’ll have twelve tabs open and still not know.\n\nBut acting without thinking things through… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I use a calendar, and I make lists—probably too many lists, honestly. I have one on my phone, one on sticky notes, and then sometimes I’ll write something down on the back of an envelope and of course that disappears.\n\nBut whether that means I have trouble organizing time or tasks… maybe? I guess it depends what you mean. Like, if it’s a workday, there are meetings already on the calendar, so that sort of organizes me from the outside. And then I’ll have these little windows between meetings where I think, okay, I’ll knock out two things, but then I end up reorganizing my inbox or looking for the right version of a document.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I do try to organize things. I just don’t always know if my system is actually working or if I’m just constantly trying to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, stress probably makes it worse, but I don’t think it only happens when I’m overwhelmed.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is stuff like, I’ll open an EPIC quality report with every intention of really going through it, or I’ll be sitting in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow the discussion, and within a few minutes my mind is just… gone somewhere else. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. But the drifting happens even when I start out calm and prepared.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus on certain types of tasks or activities than others?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. I mean, I use a calendar, and I make lists—probably too many lists, honestly. I have one on my phone, one on sticky notes, and then sometimes I’ll write something down on the back of an envelope and of course that disappears.\n\nBut whether that means I have trouble organizing time or tasks… maybe? I guess it depends what you mean. Like, if it’s a workday, there are meetings already on the calendar, so that sort of organizes me from the outside. And then I’ll have these little windows between meetings where I think, okay, I’ll knock out two things, but then I end up reorganizing my inbox or looking for the right version of a document.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I do try to organize things. I just don’t always know if my system is actually working or if I’m just constantly trying to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, stress probably makes it worse, but I don’t think it only happens when I’m overwhelmed.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is stuff like, I’ll open an EPIC quality report with every intention of really going through it, or I’ll be sitting in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow the discussion, and within a few minutes my mind is just… gone somewhere else. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. But the drifting happens even when I start out calm and prepared.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus on certain types of tasks or activities than others?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you're not actively engaged in an activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.\n\nRelaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.\n\nLike even watching TV, I’ll sometimes feel like I should be folding laundry or checking something, but then again everyone I know does that now. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying with things I really need to focus on. Like at work, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else completely.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or suddenly realize I missed the whole point of what people were discussing. Like I’ll start something with a few steps, and I’ll do the first part, maybe get the next part half set up, and then it just sort of stays there. I do try to organize things. I just don’t always know if my system is actually working or if I’m just constantly trying to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, stress probably makes it worse, but I don’t think it only happens when I’m overwhelmed.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is stuff like, I’ll open an EPIC quality report with every intention of really going through it, or I’ll be sitting in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow the discussion, and within a few minutes my mind is just… gone somewhere else. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. But the drifting happens even when I start out calm and prepared.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus on certain types of tasks or activities than others?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you're not actively engaged in an activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.\n\nRelaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.\n\nLike even watching TV, I’ll sometimes feel like I should be folding laundry or checking something, but then again everyone I know does that now. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about different things, but I don’t think anyone ever sat me down and said, “This is the diagnosis,” or treated me specifically for that.\n\nI did have a coworker once joke that everyone in management has some kind of attention problem because of the constant interruptions, which—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But no, I don’t think I’ve been formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F98.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you're not actively engaged in an activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.\n\nRelaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.\n\nLike even watching TV, I’ll sometimes feel like I should be folding laundry or checking something, but then again everyone I know does that now. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about different things, but I don’t think anyone ever sat me down and said, “This is the diagnosis,” or treated me specifically for that.\n\nI did have a coworker once joke that everyone in management has some kind of attention problem because of the constant interruptions, which—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But no, I don’t think I’ve been formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you experience similar difficulties with focus and task completion as a child?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright but careless, and my mother had to sit with me while I finished homework because I would lose track of assignments or forget books at school.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, looking back, even in elementary school I remember teachers writing things like that I was bright but careless, which always felt… frustrating, because I wasn’t trying to be careless.

And my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do, or I’d realize I’d left a book at school. Actually, I can still picture this one kitchen table we had—sorry, that’s not important—but yes, there were definitely issues with finishing assignments and keeping track of school things when I was young.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a hard time staying with things I really need to focus on. Like at work, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting fully intending to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else completely.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’ll be rereading the same numbers over and over, or suddenly realize I missed the whole point of what people were discussing. And these aren’t unimportant things — I manage health services, so I need to be tracking this stuff. I mean, I’ve always had a lot going on, and meetings can be dry, sure, but lately it’s gotten to the point where it’s affecting me enough that I thought I should finally talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself, and then that can make me feel kind of low for a bit, but not like… sad all the time. It’s more that I’m annoyed because I know I’m capable, and then I’ll lose track in a meeting and spend the rest of the afternoon thinking about it.\n\nActually, yesterday I was trying to clean up my inbox after one of those long metrics calls, and I found an email I meant to answer two weeks ago, which just sent me down this whole rabbit hole of checking other messages. But, sorry — to answer you, I haven’t really noticed feeling depressed or down in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as restless, exactly. I mean, I’ll shift around in my chair sometimes, but I think everyone does that, especially in those long conference room chairs that somehow manage to be uncomfortable no matter how you sit.\n\nI haven’t really noticed a big urge to get up and move around or anything like that. It’s more like… well, I don’t know, I’d have to think about it. Sitting still isn’t usually the thing I notice first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks that you start?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, not every single thing, but it happens enough that it’s a problem.\n\nFor example, after a committee meeting, someone might give me three or four follow-up items — like send one email, update a spreadsheet, submit something by the end of the week. I’ll usually do the first one, and maybe I’ll even start the second, like draft the email or open the spreadsheet… and then somehow it just sits there unfinished.\n\nAnd then a few days later, or sometimes longer, someone reminds me and I’m like, “Oh no, I was halfway through that.” It’s embarrassing because these are routine work things, not huge impossible projects.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or completing tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does happen outside of work too, though I notice it most at work because people are waiting on things.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just less formal. Like I’ll start something with a few steps, and I’ll do the first part, maybe get the next part half set up, and then it just sort of stays there. It’s the same pattern as after meetings — if someone gives me three or four follow-up steps, I might send the first email or start the spreadsheet, but then the rest sits unfinished until somebody reminds me.\n\nAnd then I feel bad because it’s not that I didn’t care. I just… I don’t know, I lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as especially impulsive, like I’m not usually the person who just suddenly does something wild without considering it.\n\nI mean, I might interrupt sometimes or buy something online because I’ve been meaning to replace it anyway—although half the time I spend way too long comparing reviews, which is its own whole thing. My sister can decide on a couch in ten minutes; I’ll have twelve tabs open and still not know.\n\nBut acting without thinking things through… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I use a calendar, and I make lists—probably too many lists, honestly. I have one on my phone, one on sticky notes, and then sometimes I’ll write something down on the back of an envelope and of course that disappears.\n\nBut whether that means I have trouble organizing time or tasks… maybe? I guess it depends what you mean. Like, if it’s a workday, there are meetings already on the calendar, so that sort of organizes me from the outside. And then I’ll have these little windows between meetings where I think, okay, I’ll knock out two things, but then I end up reorganizing my inbox or looking for the right version of a document.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a very direct answer. I do try to organize things. I just don’t always know if my system is actually working or if I’m just constantly trying to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying focused is triggered by feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, stress probably makes it worse, but I don’t think it only happens when I’m overwhelmed.\n\nWhat finally made me come in is stuff like, I’ll open an EPIC quality report with every intention of really going through it, or I’ll be sitting in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow the discussion, and within a few minutes my mind is just… gone somewhere else. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. But the drifting happens even when I start out calm and prepared.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to focus on certain types of tasks or activities than others?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you're not actively engaged in an activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.\n\nRelaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.\n\nLike even watching TV, I’ll sometimes feel like I should be folding laundry or checking something, but then again everyone I know does that now. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about different things, but I don’t think anyone ever sat me down and said, “This is the diagnosis,” or treated me specifically for that.\n\nI did have a coworker once joke that everyone in management has some kind of attention problem because of the constant interruptions, which—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But no, I don’t think I’ve been formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and task completion as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, looking back, even in elementary school I remember teachers writing things like that I was bright but careless, which always felt… frustrating, because I wasn’t trying to be careless.\n\nAnd my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do, or I’d realize I’d left a book at school. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. I don’t know if it’s “often” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal restlessness or an inability to relax when you're not actively engaged in an activity?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t usually think of myself as physically restless, like pacing around or needing to move all the time.\n\nRelaxing is… hard to answer, because when I’m not doing something, I usually end up thinking about what I should be doing, or I’ll remember some errand or email or something around the house. But I don’t know if that’s internal restlessness or just, you know, being an adult with too many things open at once.\n\nLike even watching TV, I’ll sometimes feel like I should be folding laundry or checking something, but then again everyone I know does that now. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly remember. I mean, I’ve talked to doctors over the years about different things, but I don’t think anyone ever sat me down and said, “This is the diagnosis,” or treated me specifically for that.\n\nI did have a coworker once joke that everyone in management has some kind of attention problem because of the constant interruptions, which—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But no, I don’t think I’ve been formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and task completion as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, looking back, even in elementary school I remember teachers writing things like that I was bright but careless, which always felt… frustrating, because I wasn’t trying to be careless.\n\nAnd my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do, or I’d realize I’d left a book at school. Actually, I can still picture this one kitchen table we had—sorry, that’s not important—but yes, there were definitely issues with finishing assignments and keeping track of school things when I was young.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think… probably, but I’m not completely sure how to compare them. Work has so many built-in deadlines and people reminding me of things, so sometimes it looks more organized than it feels. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I realize people have been talking and I missed the point entirely.\n\nAnd then I try to act normal, like I know exactly where we are, which is stressful in itself. Anything that needs sustained, detailed mental effort is harder for me.\n\nLike the dense policy reviews, or when I block off time to do a long data-analysis project—those are the things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I’m going to do them first thing, because that would be sensible, but then somehow it’s late afternoon and I’ve answered emails, checked a couple of minor things, maybe rearranged my task list… and the big thing is still sitting there.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand it or don’t care about it. It’s more like the thought of sitting with that much detailed reading and thinking feels almost physically hard to start. Then I feel ridiculous, because it’s my job, you know? But yes, those tasks are much harder to focus on than, say, something quick or interactive.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble staying focused, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I mean, it’s gotten more obvious with work because there are so many reports and meetings and all that, but when I think back, even in elementary school my teachers used to write things like “bright but careless” on my report cards.\n\nAnd my mom—she still brings this up sometimes, usually when she’s telling some story about how I was as a kid—she had to sit with me while I did homework because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do. Or I’d forget the book I needed at school, which sounds so small, but then the whole evening would become this drama of trying to figure out the assignment. So… no, I don’t think it’s brand new. I think I’ve just had better ways of covering for it as an adult, or maybe more consequences now.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or get easily upset by small things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense mood swings. I mean, I can get irritated, like anyone, especially if a meeting is going in circles or there are ten emails asking the same thing in slightly different ways.\n\nBut small things… I don’t know. Sometimes I probably react more sharply than I mean to, and then later I think, why did I sound so annoyed about that? Although that might just be work stress, honestly. Healthcare administration has this way of making even tiny issues feel like they’re attached to fifteen other problems, and then suddenly you’re talking about staffing or compliance or some report due Friday—\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess my answer is: I don’t think I have dramatic mood swings, but I can get frustrated or upset over little things sometimes. I’m not sure if it’s more than normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly 'on the go' or like you're driven to keep moving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as physically “on the go” all the time, if that makes sense. I’m not, like, pacing around the office nonstop or needing to run laps or anything.\n\nAlthough my days do feel like I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—meeting, email, someone stopping by, another meeting—and sometimes I keep moving just because if I stop, I realize how much is piled up. But that may be more the job than me. Healthcare admin kind of trains you to be in constant response mode.\n\nSo… no, I don’t know that I feel driven to keep moving physically. Maybe mentally busy, but I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you are sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, if I’m in a long meeting, I’ll shift around or adjust how I’m sitting, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness or just… those conference room chairs are awful, honestly. We have this one room where the table is too high and the screen never connects properly, and then everyone spends ten minutes troubleshooting instead of starting—\n\nSorry. The sitting-still part… I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed feeling physically fidgety in a big way. Maybe a little uncomfortable during long stretches, but I don’t know that it stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and task completion causing significant problems in your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s where it’s starting to worry me more. Outside of just feeling scattered, it’s actually affecting things.\n\nAt work, some of my delays have started holding up clinic reporting deadlines, and then other teams are waiting on me, which feels awful because I know they need my piece before they can finish theirs. And then outside my main job—well, sort of outside, I guess still health-related—I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I can’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving.\n\nI used to like doing those, actually. The workshops were kind of energizing, and people really appreciated them, especially when we did the blood pressure education sessions—sorry, that’s probably beside the point. But yes, it’s causing problems in daily responsibilities beyond just being annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’m someone who constantly cuts people off, at least I don’t think so.\n\nSometimes in meetings I might jump in if I think I understand where someone is going, especially if we’re short on time, but that’s also kind of how those meetings go—everyone’s trying to get a word in before the agenda falls apart. And there’s always that one person who turns a quick update into a whole history of the department, so then you’re trying to keep things moving—\n\nSorry, the question was whether I interrupt. I’m not really sure. I haven’t had people tell me I do that a lot, so I don’t think it’s a major thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you are doing two or more things at the same time, such as talking on the phone and working on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll have my phone up while I’m trying to finish something else, but I don’t know if that counts as doing two things or just normal work these days.\n\nHonestly, everything has gotten so layered with email, calls, messages, calendar reminders… and half the time the phone call is about the task you’re working on, so it’s hard to separate. Like if someone calls about a spreadsheet, I’ll pull up the spreadsheet while I’m talking, but that seems kind of expected.\n\nSo… I guess I do it sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a specific problem. My sister says she can just sit and watch a whole movie without touching her phone, which seems almost suspicious to me—but sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a strong “can’t relax” feeling in a way I would have named before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or treated for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly remember. But no, I don’t think I’ve been formally diagnosed or treated for it.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with focus and task completion as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, looking back, even in elementary school I remember teachers writing things like that I was bright but careless, which always felt… frustrating, because I wasn’t trying to be careless.\n\nAnd my mother used to have to sit with me while I finished homework, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d lose track of what I was supposed to do, or I’d realize I’d left a book at school. Actually, I can still picture this one kitchen table we had—sorry, that’s not important—but yes, there were definitely issues with finishing assignments and keeping track of school things when I was young.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and task completion happen in similar ways both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: I think… probably, but I’m not completely sure how to compare them.
